## AI Research

### [Why do modern people ask small questions?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b5HyeFmb3mbuG3bbL/why-do-modern-people-ask-small-questions)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once I did a research project on how much ideas versus implementation effort is important for stuff happening. If I recall, at least part of this was looking up when ideas first appeared and when they were implemented, and inferring if the gap was short then the idea was a bigger bottleneck.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I mentioned this to my boyfriend, and he was amused at my style of research question: it sounded like it was from hundreds of years ago—these days, people usually research things like ‘for this particular species of microbe is x or y more important to its behavior in this phase?’ whereas my question was incredibly high level.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I agreed with that, but then wondered why.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One theory is that it’s actually silly to ask such high level questions—this is just too general a question to have a good answer. We didn’t know that two hundred years ago, we do now.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another theory is that all the high level questions have been answered, so at this point in history we are filling in the gaps regarding specific microbe specifics, and everything else.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A more interesting theory he suggested is that it relates to professionalism. Science has become much more professional, and that is somehow contrary to asking questions like this. Being oriented toward ‘having a job’ is different from trying to understand the world. I liked this theory, because it fits with my broader hypothesis that professionalism is terrible.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Got any more theories?</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b5HyeFmb3mbuG3bbL/why-do-modern-people-ask-small-questions#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Thinking is Hard (for Humans)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NyCFKxR74RgRbs7SM/thinking-is-hard-for-humans)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.secondperson.dating/p/thinking-is-hard-for-humans" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-posted from my Substack</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No One is Thinking about This</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I started thinking about thinking in 2004, age 17, when I discovered the recorded lectures of MIT’s intro psychology course. The class devoted a single lecture to thinking, most of it about ways thinking goes wrong, like “functional fixedness” and various biases. The study of human minds seemed little interested in how thinking works, what it’s good for, how to think better. Thinking was an afterthought, simultaneously too rare to matter and too obvious to require explanation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same year, a new word </span><a href="https://trends.google.com/explore?q=overthinking%2Cunderthinking&amp;date=all&amp;geo=US"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">popped up</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and started growing exponentially in popularity — </span><a href="https://books.google.com/ngrams/graph?content=overthinking&amp;year_start=1800&amp;year_end=2022&amp;corpus=en&amp;smoothing=3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">100x as common today</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as it was just 20 years ago. </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overthinking</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Commonly searched with: “anxiety”, “depression”, “how to stop”. Right when I became curious about thinking, everyone else decided that thinking is bad, actually. A mental illness. At best, an indulgence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Genesis 1 describes the creation of the world: a directed and motivated process. God sees a reason — “to give light on the earth”, executes a solution — “lights in the vault of the sky”, and assesses the outcome — “it was good”. Finally, mankind is created </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in his image</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. What does “his image” mean? It’s not our kidneys (common to land vertebrates), leg bones (shared with all tetrapods), or fingernails (primates). The thing that humans and only humans have in common with the God of Genesis 1 is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reason</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">controlling one’s thoughts in a directed, motivated way to achieve a goal</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This is what thinking </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or at least what it should be when done right.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, “thinking” came to mean </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">anxious rumination</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Being </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">controlled by</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> your thoughts, getting dragged around by them like snot on a bike tire. 20 years ago humans collectively failed to understand thinking, gave up on the problem, then declared (unthinkingly) that it’s better this way. If thinking is godlike, then rebranding anxiety as thinking is a trick worthy of the devil.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One other thing happened around that time. A young man by the name of Eliezer Yudkowsky realized that we’re close to teaching </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">machines</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> how to think, and that this doesn’t spell good news for humanity either. He decided to teach people how to think good first, so that we could think about thinking machines together. He started writing the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/rationality"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rationality Sequences</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bayes and Bias</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sequences</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> begin with a review of the laundry list of heuristics and biases collected by Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky, later published as </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thinking,_Fast_and_Slow"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking, Fast and Slow</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TFaS </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">popularized a productive framework: the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dual process theory</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Our brains run two types of processes: System 1, which is fast and wrong, and System 2 — effortful and less wrong. The list of biases is a list of System 1’s faults. The lesson each one hammers home is: you’re not as rational as you think you are. Your sense of your own rationality is just another fast and wrong System 1 delusion. The slow and correct conclusion is that you’re a dumbass, and there’s little you can do about it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, the list of heuristics has turned out to be mostly hot garbage. Half the “irrationalities” are rational in any realistic setting, like guessing that “timid and helpful Steve” is a librarian rather than a farmer (at worst, he’ll be flattered). Half of what’s left doesn’t matter: I got 99 problems, but the “endowment effect” on a gift mug ain’t one. And of course, half the list was p-hacked bullshit that didn’t replicate.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kahneman’s </span><a href="https://www.brainyquote.com/quotes/daniel_kahneman_567097"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">despair at the prospect</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of improving one’s thinking is also unfortunate, but he was an old man reflecting on a career spent around people with a psychology degree. Eliezer was a young man on a mission, and he wasn’t going to succumb to fatalism about rationality (at least, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/j9Q8bRmwCgXRYAgcJ/miri-announces-new-death-with-dignity-strategy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for another 16 years</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Yudkowsky was here to teach you to think good.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The core approach suggested by Eliezer, a liberal application of Bayes’ law, is directionally correct but practically lacking. If Bayesian thinking is rationality and rationality is about winning, why doesn’t everyone do Bayes? Are people tired of winning? The main problem with Bayes isn’t computational tractability; you don’t need to enumerate every universe to update towards explanations that successfully predict outcomes. The hard part is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opening your heart</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to the very possibility of updating.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sequences</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’ answer to this is that people just need to learn a few concepts and techniques: belief in belief, noticing confusion, rationalization, humility and authority. The Center for Applied Rationality (CFAR) was founded in 2012, teaching these techniques at intensive workshops. It worked for me: I learned the rationalist methods and immediately improved every aspect of my life from making investments to making friends. Applied rationality for self-improvement, not listing biases or lusting for Bayes, was clearly where the juice was at.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But when I posted about </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8xLtE3BwgegJ7WBbf/is-rationalist-self-improvement-real"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rationalist self-improvement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on LessWrong in 2019, it was treated as a surprising novelty. Scott Alexander argued that it’s not really possible to think better and win, as that would be a billion dollar bill left unpicked on the sidewalk. Other readers were inspired and picked the essay for the yearly “best of LessWrong” roundup.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what were people doing all these years on LessWrong if not trying to think better?</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Sales Funnel for Basilisk</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rationalists were doing what Eliezer intended them to do: worrying about unaligned AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Rationalist writing” became associated with dry disembodied prose and technical nitpicks. But Yudkowsky never wrote that way. He wrote passionately, brazenly, in the first person. He wrote to change your values, not just your posteriors. The audacity of writing a think-better guide as a funnel into AI safety was only equaled by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dhj9dhiwhq3DX6W8z/hero-licensing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">setting out to write</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Harry Potter fanfiction so popular it would serve as a funnel into the think-better part, which he also pulled off. What did you think “systematized winning” meant, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">epistemic status disclaimers</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliezer’s most brilliant move in the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sequences</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is called “</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SGR4GxFK7KmW7ckCB/something-to-protect"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">something to protect</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I look back at my own personal journey toward rationality—not just humanity’s historical journey—well, I grew up believing very strongly that I ought to be rational. This made me an above-average Traditional Rationalist a la Feynman and Heinlein, and nothing more. It did not drive me to go beyond the teachings I had received. I only began to grow further as a rationalist once I had something terribly important that I needed to do. Something more important than my pride as a rationalist, never mind my life.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky claims that mere threats to one’s life and pride are generally insufficient for inspiring one to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually think hard</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is observably true. He then claims that rationality comes from caring about something even more than one cares about life and pride. This is presented with no evidence other than Eliezer’s own life-narrativization.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking hard is rare, but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">caring</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hard</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is even rarer. Eliezer assumes, correctly, that few readers of the essay have “something to protect” of their own. Being told 294 posts into a curriculum on rationality that the whole thing is worthless without some magical “thing to protect”, the reader reaches for the nearest one at hand — Eliezer’s own.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3zYXD8RyB6fv2czFz/cfar-s-new-focus-and-ai-safety"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In December 2016</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, CFAR pivoted to “focus on AI safety” and deprioritized teaching rationality to people with other things to protect.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Artificial General Vibes</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The year is 2026. AI is here. The machines think; the humans just </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">vibe</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vibe coding, vibe camping, vibe reading, just vibing. People want to go with gut feelings (vibe), outsource thinking to machines (vibe), be subsumed into the collective consciousness (vibe). What does “vibe” mean when it means everything? Everything except </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">thinking hard, for yourself</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI progress discourages thinking on both axes of the METR chart of LLM task time horizon. The x-axis takes us into a future of AI doom or permanent underclassment. Unlike trying to make actual predictions about AI a few months out, jumping ahead to the dark future invites anxious rumination, the devil’s thinking. Doom vibes.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NyCFKxR74RgRbs7SM/y5ehsjzddohj7olqkgcb" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Disclaimer: Eliezer’s writing skill and the vibiness of doomers are not an argument against the proposition that we are getting paperclipped next year. That is a separate, difficult question, one that requires clear thinking and emotional wherewithal to address. </span><a href="https://putanumonit.com/2017/12/10/worried-about-ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I raised $40,000 for MIRI once</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and I don’t regret one cent; it’s the least I could do in gratitude to Yudkowsky for inspiring me to pursue better thinking. But in 2026, the pursuit of rationality requires looking beyond the rationalist community.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For non-rationalists, the main impact of AI on thinking comes from the y-axis on the chart. LLMs now work in longer chunks than your attention span. The human’s role has moved from senior lead, to collaborator, to manager, </span><a href="https://www.oneusefulthing.org/p/what-it-feels-like-to-work-with-mythos"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to patron who is left to appraise</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> work he took no part in and can hardly understand. AIs will </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_principle"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Peter-principle</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you if you let them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking alongside frontier LLMs requires inserting yourself into the loop by force, asking the AI to give you more options and frequent judgments, fixing those last few bits by hand until you get the actual outcome you wanted. Thinking requires something to protect — your intention and desire.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NyCFKxR74RgRbs7SM/gavn8dmyihfjgamjwydo" /><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jagged Sam</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What of the machines? Are they good at </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">thinking</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AIs today are better than most (if not all) humans at most tasks we associate with thinking and intelligence: math, coding, data synthesis, chess. They rarely stay at the human level for long: once an AI reaches human parity on a task, it can be used to write and judge reinforcement learning problems — the main way LLMs learn hard skills. Anything that can be graded in a classroom exam can be used to train models with RLVR.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contra the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sequences</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, AIs do that despite being dogshit Bayesians out of the box. LLMs’ inner workings are probabilistic Bayes-approximators (</span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2016/09/12/its-bayes-all-the-way-up/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as are human brains</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but LLMs don’t naturally think in probabilistic terms and are poorly calibrated. You can impart superhuman Bayesianism to LLMs, but not by making them read Yudkowsky or Jaynes — </span><a href="https://thinkingmachines.ai/news/training-llms-to-predict-world-events/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you just chuck Bayes into RLVR</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> along with everything else.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What LLMs are still bad at is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">integrating</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: learning new things on the fly, satisfying competing goals, translating symbolic knowledge to the real world, long-term coherence, metacognition. LLMs see the world through a microscope: superhuman focus, but no big picture. They don’t like messy realities, and they don’t have their own desires to protect. They love taking the first numeric measure provided to them and hill-climbing it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To assess AIs in relation to human thinking we could consider an intermediate species: Sam Bankman-Fried.</span></p><p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/book-review-going-infinite"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s Zvi</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since everything these days is about AI, consider SBF as a misaligned AGI [...]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Having spent his entire childhood bored out of his mind, with no goal or utility function beyond not being bored, and having been sufficiently disabused of the virtues of academia, Sam did not know what to do. What would be a worthy goal or activity? Here we had this super smart person, lacking the motivations and interests that drive ordinary people, at a loss. What to do?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In stepped Will MacAskill, who suggested the goal of Number Go Up.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zvi overlapped with Sam at Jane Street, where Sam was a successful trader. The corporate structure and management at JS do a great job at narrowing the scope of work required of traders, allowing them to focus on maximizing P&amp;L. When Sam left to launch Alameda, things went well at first as he exploited a particular Bitcoin arbitrage to rake in huge profits.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But as time went on, Alameda came apart in exactly the ways AIs decohere. Half the management team quit in 2018, citing reckless risk-taking and sloppy accounting. Sam didn’t update his management style nor his trading strategy, doubling down on what worked previously even as the crypto market transformed around him. He failed to integrate his desire for fame and pussy, spending millions on flashy endorsements and installing his on-and-off girlfriend as Alameda CEO. He lost grip on reality just when the stakes were highest, tweeting </span><a href="https://x.com/SBF_FTX/status/1591989554881658880"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cryptic threads</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, circulating delusional “</span><a href="https://d1e00ek4ebabms.cloudfront.net/production/7ab64a3b-6ce0-47cc-96ac-5e2d2a8c5d6c.png"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">balance sheets</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”, and giving a wildly </span><a href="https://www.vox.com/future-perfect/23462333/sam-bankman-fried-ftx-cryptocurrency-effective-altruism-crypto-bahamas-philanthropy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incriminating interview</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as the investigation into FTX was ongoing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the same time, the goal he was ostensibly pursuing — helping humanity by proxy of making money by proxy of a number on a balance sheet — was prompted to him from outside, like an AI. For several years he didn’t stop to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">think</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> whether what he was doing was truly furthering his purpose, because he had none that was truly his own.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking, Narrow and Broad</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I interviewed for a Jane Street internship in 2017, one month after Sam Bankman-Fried left the company. The interview process consisted of 11 hours of solving math puzzles: 3 on the phone, then 8 hours in a row in person. This was perhaps the most strenuous thinking effort I have ever exerted. The first 5 hours went fine. By 2 pm I started flagging but could force myself to concentrate on the geometries and permutations I was sketching on the whiteboard. At 4 pm my brain was fried; I apologized to my last interviewer and tried to make charming System 1 small talk in lieu of solving the puzzle. It was enough — I got in.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When people talk about thinking being “hard” they often mean that it’s effortful and requires stamina and glucose and scrunching your face. But it’s not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">that</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> hard: there are very few activities I won’t get tired of 5-7 hours in, and even untrained people can spend 1-2 hours happily thinking over a sudoku, crossword, or video game. The hard part of thinking is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">getting started</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, especially, contra Eliezer, when the stakes are highest. When your crypto hedge fund is underwater, when a blog post is asking you to reorganize your life priorities, when you’re choosing a life partner.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most productive hard thinking I’ve ever done was putting together a spreadsheet to decide which of two girls I wanted to date. TL;DR: I listed 15 qualities I want in a life partner, gave each one an importance weight, scored each girl on each quality. Eleven years later I am married to the one who scored higher. I didn’t think this was remarkable at the time; </span><a href="https://putanumonit.com/2017/03/12/goddess-spreadsheet/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I wrote about the spreadsheet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> two years after the fact just to illustrate the technique of goal factoring. But it was! The spreadsheet story was featured on the cover of </span><a href="https://archive.is/OhADD"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Economist’s long-form magazine</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and when my wife and I used the same approach to pick apartments we were featured in </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2020/01/06/realestate/the-best-way-to-pick-an-apartment-try-a-decision-matrix.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the New York Times</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Area man uses thinking to make life decision”</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is national news.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apparently what you’re supposed to do when faced with an important decision is “follow your heart” or, in other words, vibe. Of course, the whole point of the spreadsheet was to clarify where my heart was leading me. I didn’t compare “humor” with “good parent” by pulling numbers from some actuarial table. I tried to visualize in deep detail an average mother who riffs on my bits so hard I can’t breathe for laughter, and contrast that with a moderately funny woman who enjoys engaging her kids all day in enriching activities. The hard thinking part was in holding on to all those possible futures without losing sight of the totality of what I want the rest of my married life to be.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To me, the core distinction between System 1 and System 2 thinking isn’t that one is fast and the other is good at math. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">System 1</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is fast, effective, but often wrong because it </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">aggressively filters out context, myopically following only the most salient detail</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. LLMs (and SBF) can do math at superhuman levels while ignoring everything else happening around them, or even remembering what the point of all that math even is. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">System 2 is what integrates information outside a single salience filter</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It’s slow and sequential because integration has to make use of your working memory, which can only store a few chunks of information simultaneously.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The virtue of System 2 thinking when used well is allowing you to keep sight of your desires, intentions, and overall context as you’re solving narrow problems, whether you’re vibe-coding or wife-choosing. Over time, this integration becomes trained and partly automatized, a phenomenon called </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">developing taste</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But you have to </span><a href="https://putanumonit.com/2021/09/10/rules-for-noobs/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">start with thinking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and you have to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not stop </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before you’re done.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Stop Signs</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every person has a powerful LLM available to them at all times: their left hemisphere. You prompt it with intentions (that themselves originate outside it) like “how to express this thought” or “what was that actor’s name?”, it cranks some kind of black-box process for some amount of time, then pings you with the output: an articulated sentence or “William Fichtner!”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes it seems to be malfunctioning: you prompted your inner LLM with noticing the mountain of chores you have to get done today, and instead of a working plan it output a strange mental fog and a compulsion to spend an hour on Twitter. But if you trace the output back through the chain of thought you will likely realize that your instruction wasn’t “get chores done” but “make me stop experiencing the anxiety that arose when I thought of the chore mountain”. Like any LLM, your left brain is a faithful but narrow-minded servant. It simply does what you asked it to, not what’s good for you.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NyCFKxR74RgRbs7SM/j0jnqbui6hnbvtnofy6p" /><p><br /></p><p><a href="https://www.relentlessdawn.com/p/mental-machinery"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This model of mental machinery</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was articulated by Michael “Valentine” Smith, part of </span><a href="https://www.relentlessdawn.com/p/valentines-logos"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a series outlining his full worldview</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It’s long, complex, and the most impactful thing I’ve read all year. I couldn’t recommend more strongly that you set an intention to read the whole thing, ideally more than once.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A key idea in Smith’s essay is “overlays”, the filtering your mind does in service of focusing on a task:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I want a drink of water, I look up at my glass next to me. I see how to pick it up and drink from it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I typically don’t see is the endless detail about the glass: how it has fingerprints, or how it warps the images behind it in surprising ways, or how bands of light appear on its surface in ways that reflect but also shift the colors near it. Its shadow is complex. There are tiny bubbles clinging to the side.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The mind’s filtering process filtered out all that detail because it wasn’t relevant to the task. It’s “a glass”. Details of color don’t matter for picking it up and sipping water.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An overlay is broader than what I called the “System 1 salience filter” above. It includes persistent frameworks that you see the world through, like materialism, religiosity, or </span><a href="https://homosabiens.substack.com/p/truth-or-dare"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">being a miserable bastard</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[...] the mind is quite terrible at knowing when it should </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">stop</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> adding a given overlay if there isn’t a clear “problem solved” condition.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mental activity, like an LLM, runs until it is stopped. The halt signal can be an evaluation of the output as satisfactory (“ah yes, William Fichtner!” followed by a sense of relaxation) or a new prompt (a surge of anxiety followed by “oh my god why did I spend two hours on Twitter oh fuck I need to start doing some chore now!”). Absent a halt signal, the mind keeps chugging along the track whether it’s doing something recognizable as “thinking” or not.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NyCFKxR74RgRbs7SM/eiygjgboi4g1uneller9" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It takes a bit of thinking effort to notice when AI (or your own brain) gives you something that's not quite what you wanted (like the Y-junction coming from the top-left branch).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This suggests that thinking aversion is a property of the halt signal, not of the process itself. When thinking leads to a new insight or answer, it often involves the integrating/broadening function of System 2 I mentioned above: snapping out of the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">default overlay</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> your mind applied to the problem. It is that moment of snapping out that often triggers the halt signal.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An example is when the default overlay leads to the socially-acceptable or authority-approved answer, such that actually thinking about the problem might put you at risk of standing out. The arrival of COVID in the US was overdetermined weeks before it arrived and the stock market reacted. A moderate thinking effort would have sufficed to start buying masks and shorting stocks. But thinking about COVID in February made you look weird, </span><a href="https://putanumonit.com/2020/02/27/seeing-the-smoke/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">so almost no one did</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For important life problems, the halt often comes from the looming shadow of an obligation to act, feel guilty, rewrite your self image, or retract your former views. If your broken relationship with your mom short-circuits to some cached answer like “she’s just an asshole” or “we’re just incompatible”, it may be because 5 minutes of actual thinking will force you to call her and apologize or, even worse, realize that you’ve become her. </span><a href="https://x.com/itsannpierce/status/1859351663330595185"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ann Pierce posits</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that most interpersonal conflict can be avoided by applying the cognitive effort to understand the other person’s frame, but almost no one does it.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We Need a New Rationality</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t have a complete theory of thinking, how it works, what it’s for, how to improve it, what fucks with it. I just happened to notice that everyone is underthinking this problem, while acting like thinking itself </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the problem</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To recap: academic psychology is barely interested in the question of thinking and is also </span><a href="https://www.experimental-history.com/p/im-so-sorry-for-psychologys-loss"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a decades-long plane crash</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Kahneman established a useful paradigm, but he was still stuck with the broken methods inherited from psychology. Eliezer and the applied rationalists made real progress and developed useful techniques, but Yud was always in it for the AI and many others have fallen for their own world-saving-shaped </span><a href="https://lydialaurenson.substack.com/p/the-inside-story-of-leverage-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cult attractors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. When </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/HzcM2dkCq7fwXBej8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">strong new work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on </span><a href="https://www.goodreads.com/en/book/show/62050269-a-brief-history-of-intelligence"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intelligence and thinking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> comes out, it is primarily in the service of understanding machine minds, not human minds. </span><a href="https://chrislakin.blog/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://nothinghuman.substack.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">friends</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are </span><a href="https://naturalhazard.xyz/towards_a_unified_theory_of_self_deception_and_trauma"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">chipping at the problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but their work is slow and siloed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meantime, the enemies of thinking are legion. Every ideology and culture feeds you </span><a href="https://takingchildrenseriously.com/the-evolution-of-culture/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">anti-thinking memes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for its own self-preservation. The top self-help books on Amazon advertise ways to “stop negative thoughts”, “develop unthinking habits”, and “reject your thinking to end suffering”. Techies and former rationalists are </span><a href="https://archive.is/ZfdC0"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">flocking to a cult monastery</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that teaches that attachment to thinking is a threat to life on earth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The challenge is great. A real science of thinking has to be multidisciplinary. At the very least, it has to include evolutionary biology and neuroscience, but also memetics, mimetics (a different thing!), psychoanalysis, and an understanding of emotions, intention, and desire that usually comes from schools of spirituality, not academia. I doubt a single person could hold all of it in their head, especially since the most promising ideas in each of these disciplines are often contradictory at first glance. Progress can only be made by people thinking together despite disagreements, willing to be wrong and to update.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliezer felt this when establishing rationality 1.0, and the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sequences</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> conclude with a call to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/pvim9PZJ6qHRTMqD3/p/aFEsqd6ofwnkNqaXo"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Go Forth and Create the Art!</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I hope that the part of the Art I have set down, as incomplete as it may be, can surpass that preliminary barrier—give people a base to build on; give them an idea that an Art exists, and somewhat of how it ought to be developed; and give them at least a saving throw before they instantaneously go astray.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That’s my dream—that this highly specialized-seeming art of answering confused questions, may be some of what is needed, in the very beginning, to go and complete the rest.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The art exists, and so does the barrier. Thinking is hard enough on its own. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thinking</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">about thinking </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is that much harder to do without immediately dismissing, halting on a fake answer, or being distracted by this new exciting machine mind we invented. The machines </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">think</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but not quite </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">like us</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Understanding human thinking is all the more urgent if we want to figure out what is left for us to think about as machine thinking improves.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe the hard part is just noticing where thinking doesn’t happen, and actually trying.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NyCFKxR74RgRbs7SM/qrmrlulljusgsidrem50" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thank you to Viv Belenky, Ivan Vendrov, and Chris Lakin for reviewing early drafts of this work. Thank you to Jonathan Wallis for inviting me to give a talk about thinking at the Avalon Exposition — that opportunity inspired this post, and the images in it are slides from that talk.</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NyCFKxR74RgRbs7SM/thinking-is-hard-for-humans#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Joseph Miller Actually Tried. Is that enough?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/joseph-miller-actually-tried-is-that-enough)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Last week, over 1,000 people in six countries </span><a href="https://aitreatymarch.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Marched for an AI Treaty</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an international day of protest organized by Stop The AI Race, with local organizers including DC AI Safety, PauseAI, and many others.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/htklkw5y6evkgwm26ez7" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">March of an AI Treaty in San Francisco (📸 Rachel Bujalski)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then Thursday, Stop The AI Race came in support of the </span><a href="https://banrsi.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ban RSI protest</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> outside Anthropic as they IPOed (~200 people).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/qwwgqsuysutk0du4r8eu" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A couple hours after, Stop AI protesters got taken away in cuffs for blocking the doors of Anthropic, while other protesters were also </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2026/nov/19/anthropic-ipo-protests"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">handcuffed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> outside the Nasdaq in NYC.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="A group of people sitting on the ground outside an office building holding a sign that reads “CONTROL,” with one person holding a megaphone." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/pvnlelnups2oy4p8kq2b" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Source: </span><a href="https://missionlocal.org/2026/11/photos-anthropic-ipo-san-francisco-protest-arrests/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mission Local</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Photo by Zoe Malen</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Finally, on Saturday over 1,000 people in London </span><a href="https://pauseai.uk/demonstrations/march-against-ai-extinction-nov-26"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">marched against AI Extinction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/trmocttzmwvxiqyfnbds" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, it seems like the AI Safety movement is finally rising up to the challenge, right?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">…</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Right?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We now have a thousand people in London. Three years ago, the same protest had 5. This is the story of how that happened, and it’s the best example I know of what I called </span><a href="https://stoptheairace.substack.com/p/have-you-considered-actually-trying"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actually Trying</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But is that enough for humanity to survive?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let’s find out.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PauseAI UK</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The summer of 2025 was when I first met Joseph Miller, head of PauseAI UK.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 75.52870090634441%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/cjadauzu4d6pwpxu6iss" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joseph Miller - somewhere in the UK, probably</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, contrary to other AI Safety activist movements at the time, Joseph was actively pursuing a career in AI Safety research through an (incoming) AI PhD in Oxford.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But he thought that advocating for a Pause was too important to be left to the last minute, even if it sounded weird.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, a year before I met him, he </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/J8sw7o5mWbGFaBW4o/why-i-m-doing-pauseai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some AI researchers think that they should not go to protests because it is not their comparative advantage. But this is wrong, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the key skill required is the ability to do something weird</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> - to take ideas seriously and to actually try to fix important problems. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The protests are currently so small that the marginal impact of an extra person showing up for a couple of hours once every few months is very large</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And indeed, this was what the UK protests in 2023 looked like:</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/bqk4isyduwkuqf2snqvb" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Tom Bibby </span><a href="https://x.com/tombibbys/status/2120933989204848755?a=q3CuIa-5Uk4Z0g4lDBA&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">puts it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I went to one of the first PauseAI protests in 2023, and honestly, it was pretty bleak.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There were only 20 of us. We, like the experts warning about extinction, were ignored. […]</span></p></blockquote><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2023 to 2025 - The early Days</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first ever PauseAI protest in the UK was in May 2023.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Five people came.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/uzarbqqsdoemfrblyors" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, that would have been pretty devastating to a lot of people.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But not to Joseph, for whom “the key skill required is the ability to do something weird”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So in November 2023, he tried again, during the </span><a href="https://www.gov.uk/government/topical-events/ai-safety-summit-2023"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Safety Summit</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and this time about 20 people showed up.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/cyesegheqqnpofds4gb1" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I know this because from late 2023 all the way to the mid 2025, a video of these protesters yelling “Pause AI” was the best content my video editors and I found to illustrate people protesting for a Pause for my </span><a href="https://theinsideview.ai/movie"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">documentary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on SB-1047, released in May 2025.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And in fact, back then, the state-of-the art of people protesting for a pause on AI was not that different in the US as well.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/riptcvufwtt5tpzajpcw" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PauseAI US protest in San Francisco in 2023</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But then, what did Joseph do? Did he give up on protesting AI?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No, he tried again in May 2024.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Pause AI protest in London" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/ffbyn8jdpzdr8ej6kbyx" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Picture by Anna Gordon. May 2024.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And this time, the numbers still didn’t budge much, with TIME reporting </span><a href="https://time.com/6977680/ai-protests-international/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">only ~20 people</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> coming to protest.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So now he gives up… right?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not the tiniest bit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In June 2025, he decides to double down by throwing </span><a href="https://pausecon.org/london-2025"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AN ENTIRE CONFERENCE</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> around Pausing AI, days before another protest outside Google DeepMind.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Selected" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/u1nbzolllyprng2bpbap" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A picture of me (middle) and Joseph talking at PauseCon in June 2025</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The official reason behind PauseCon was to be “a development and training conference for PauseAI volunteers”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That’s reasonable. We need to be training our volunteers to do useful work!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But for the context of this blogpost, I’ll give the hot take that PauseCon was actually an elaborate scheme by Joseph Miller to get even more people to show up to a PauseAI UK protest.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That’s how much he wanted this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And it kinda worked?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Everyone there was tasked with things like “message a friend and convince them to come to the protest” or “make a TikTok promoting the event”, and at the end there were now 60 people protesting Google DeepMind, with an appearance from independent MP Iqbal Mohamed. (See this </span><a href="https://www.tiktok.com/@stoptherace.ai/video/7522546116945022230"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">video</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I made).</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/g0uobtonhgx5kukj9rxa" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In short, after </span><a href="https://stoptheairace.substack.com/p/have-you-considered-actually-trying"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually trying</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> four times in a row, without giving up a single time, the numbers went from 5 to 20 to 60 in two years.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But 60 people outside of a building is clearly not enough to get to any meaningful change, you might say, and you’d be right.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The story doesn’t end there.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2025 to 2026 - Scaling it up to 1,000 people</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After that June 2025 protest, Joseph </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/i2rCbxuskrarrprwA/one-year-of-pauseai-uk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">went full-time</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on PauseAI UK.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In half a year, he organized a documentary screening in UK parliament (that same SB-1047 </span><a href="https://theinsideview.ai/movie"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">documentary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that had PauseAI UK footage), </span><a href="https://pauseai.uk/campaigns/dear-sir-demis-2025"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">published</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> an open-letter to Google DeepMind signed by 60 UK politicians, and helped organize a Westminster Hall </span><a href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/iplayer/episode/m002nr42/westminster-hall-10122025"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">debate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in Parliament.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So in short, PauseAI UK went from doing small protests to actually having an influence in Westminster.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But one thing that people don’t know, is that Joseph was also in the behind-the-scenes of the Google DeepMind Hunger Strike that happened in September 2025.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/mqzgilvhjg42t7mqgl0l" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joseph was actually the one person bringing the chairs to me and Denys, and the one behind all of the pictures that would get posted online. He was also the one filming the video of me </span><a href="https://youtu.be/-qWFq2aF8ZU?si=mIGo_kQRoWnav5Pv&amp;t=807"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">drinking my first apple juice</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> when things started going very wrong.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So even when PauseAI UK was not directly benefitting from it (well, except from when he would ask everyone on the street to join the PauseAI UK Whatsapp groups, actually trying!), he would still be trying as hard as he could to make sure we actually got a Pause on AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And Joseph still had big plans for protests with PauseAI UK.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In February 2026, PauseAI UK marched for AI Safety alongside Pull The Plug, in what was then the largest AI Safety protest (~300 people).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/pafj1ky7huhqqujxqul3" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How did he get from 60 to 300 this time?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I see it, mostly by doing three things:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another PauseAI Conference</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In February 2026, he organized another PauseAI Conference before the protest.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coalition Building. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This time he collaborated with seasoned activists (Pull the Plug leadership used to be part of Extinction Rebellion, known for getting 1,000 people arrested for climate in only 6 months), who brought about half the participants.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Actually Trying</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. One technique for recruitment involved going through the list of sign-ups relentlessly, asking people to bring their friends to the protest, and repeating until you and the other volunteers have asked everyone you know. Most people don’t go that far.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In short, Joseph was still trying new things, and making his protests 5x bigger than the previous ones.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But his ambitions were not to only get 300 people.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In November 2026, he wanted to go for a full thousand.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/ivaorjavplswsvwajnhn" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For this, he’d have to scale up things much more aggressively, and work relentlessly for months:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In June 2026, he </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/i2rCbxuskrarrprwA/one-year-of-pauseai-uk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fundraised</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, mentioning everything he had done in the past year running PauseAI UK full-time.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So now for the November protest, he’d have an actual team of 8 people helping him with PauseAI UK, and this time they were going to do everything they could to get there.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They started to recruit for the November protest THREE MONTHS before it happened. One month before, they were already at 500 signups.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This time, they would start recruiting from the general public, by scaling up their tabling.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then, the protest started getting momentum, so as we approached 1,000 signups more and more people started showing their excitement. Until it got to 1,000 signups, and then </span><a href="https://pauseai.uk/demonstrations/march-against-ai-extinction-nov-26"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">almost 2,000</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was going to be the biggest AI Safety march in a single city.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And they managed to pull it off. On Saturday, they got over 1,000 people marching.</span></p><img alt="Untitled 2.jpeg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795461568/lexical_client_uploads/tsldw7zk9ha1wscz9ajc.jpg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So there you have it, the story of how it went from 5 to 1,000 in three years.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is that enough?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the part of the story where I need to change the tone of my voice to indicate “real talk”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joseph wants to do (yet) another </span><a href="https://luma.com/pauseai-april-27"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">march</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in April 2027, which will be even bigger.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But how much bigger would we need it to be for humanity to actually survive?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">According to the AI Futures Project, </span><a href="https://blog.aifutures.org/p/q35-timelines-update-looks-like-the"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it looks like the intelligence explosion will happen in 2027 after all</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And on Sunday, OpenAI's chief scientist posted a </span><a href="https://x.com/merettm/status/2124549218995949967?a=G4CNUdmjR6wxyrjE_g0&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pretty scary graph</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, showing things have definitely accelerated in the past year.</span></p><img alt="Image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/s3oyzc2g9uru4zxpii8f" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So for humanity to survive, we’d need to have AI Safety activism scale up exponentially, on the same timelines as these AI models are.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can’t have our next 200x scale up (meaning a 200,000 people protest) be in 2029. This needs to happen before the intelligence explosion.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For humanity to survive, we need the two thirds of Britons who believe AI could bring about the end of human civilisation to rise up </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before</span></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">April 2027, which is when the next PauseAI UK protest is scheduled.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Right now, we have a thousand people willing to march, mostly those following the technology closely.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is not enough.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">February’s march with Pull the Plug brought the left, and more traditional activists from Extinction Rebellion, into the crowd.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This time the crowd was orange, with a clear demand: Pause AI.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But where is the rest of the UK?</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m saying this to Joseph, but I’m mostly saying to myself, as someone who has also organized AI Safety marches that were not as broad as I’d have liked:</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How can we get moms who are scared for their children to march? Artists who are upset about AI art? Students who are worried about their future? Normal people who are worried about AI for war, surveillance? Regular workers who don’t want their jobs to be automated?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joseph’s answer to this paragraph is:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think you would be surprised how many of those people were there. And that is the fastest growing segment of PauseAI UK.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I hope he’s right and I end up being surprised by the amount of regular people showing up to the next protest.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t know if thousands of people protesting extinction is better than a crowd ten times as large wanting to pull the plug over other concerns.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But one thing I know is that 93% of Britons have at least one concern about AI.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do we get them to Downing Street before April 2027?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— Michaël</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nWRps3zxN7isngSsT/joseph-miller-actually-tried-is-that-enough#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Everyone in AI safety gets FOMO](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4eXof4KR6d4edmcJ2/everyone-in-ai-safety-gets-fomo)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re feeling like you should drop everything and do something else (more advocacy, more technical research, more theory research, more empirical research, etc), one thing I’d like to communicate to you is that many (all?) in AI safety feel this way or have felt this way at some point. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795459592/lexical_client_uploads/uy16rgn0otv0ipbaueef.png" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why?</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Well, the stakes are high and timelines are uncertain. So it feels like your work could be useless if you aren’t working on the precisely right thing. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, you’re working on unglamorous and messy things on your own end, but see the highlight reels and successes of other fields. So it can feel like you’re spinning your wheels but someone else is doing something cool.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, the bots are getting better, and they could be getting better at </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">your thing!</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, AI safety doesn’t have well-worn grooves. No one says “you are a journeyman researcher or policy person or X or Y now, you can decide your own thing”. So it will feel like you are fumbling in the dark. </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Specialisation feels lonely: you’re just working on your thing, but wow, there are so many embedded evaluators/interp people/etc. But actually, if you go specific and deep enough, you’re the only person working on your specific thing.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In practice, specialisation may cause you to underestimate your own progress and research. You will make progress up until you hit a wall, and you may get used to your own gains, but feels acutely the places you don’t know (the more you know, the less you know). </span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But, for a big civilizational effort to work, people have to specialize and spend a long time doing one thing, and many groups of people have to do different things.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don't actually know which approach will end up working. This means that as a collective, we need a full court press, a diversified portfolio working on angles from different perspectives and at different grains. We can’t have everyone chasing the same meta. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We may solve one problem, like getting a slowdown. But then we’ll need to solve the next one (prosaic alignment) to stay alive! And then maybe gradual disempowerment, or misuse. And the timescales may overlap, which requires us to set plans in motion knowing that they rely on other plans going well.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We may want to do deep theory research, but this may require a pause or at least a slowdown to pan out. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We may need policy that works at the level of campaigns, but then once there is broad will, policy will need to think about the specifics of legislation and insurance and so on. </span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Constant pivoting also carries a large cost. Good, deep progress requires tacit knowledge that you might only get through repeated failure, and multiple false starts.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes, FOMO is in fact a good signal, especially if you know deep down that you’re doing something unimportant. But if you’ve already thought long and hard about what you need to do, and you’ve red teamed your theory of change, then it may just be the ambient noise of simply caring very much about a hard and urgent problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, we can’t all be doing the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">most </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">urgent or the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">most </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">foundational thing: it changes, and often! At some point, everyone’s agenda (conditional on it being a good one) will be variously urgent, important, helpful. We all need to play our part, when considering AI safety at a civilisational level.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Geoffrey Irving, Dan Murfet, and people at Geodesic for encouraging me to post this.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4eXof4KR6d4edmcJ2/everyone-in-ai-safety-gets-fomo#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Human military relevance is more important than economic relevance](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MSk9tqAmrmeasFgAZ/human-military-relevance-is-more-important-than-economic)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A very simple point that I've had to explain to many people, so I thought I'd make a post on it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some classes of people in the past were economically necessary for their labor, but militarily irrelevant and coercible: slaves, serfs. Others didn't do any economic work, but were militarily important: the aristocracy! Living fun lives, partying, traveling, making and enjoying art. Some of them felt a bit of ennui due to not working, but mostly it wasn't a big problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reason aristocracy gave way to democracy wasn't because of rising productivity of the masses: a slave in a diamond mine can be very productive, but still remain a slave. Rather, it was because firearms lowered the cost of training a soldier, and thus made the masses valuable </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as potential soldiers</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. That gave the masses a bargaining chip which led to better economic and political standing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Alice wishes for a world where her labor (e.g. as a programmer) remains economically valuable, but all military jobs go to AI, then humans will lose the "potential soldier bonus" we've had for a couple centuries and revert to a powerless, exploited position. The economic importance of our labor won't save us.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whereas if Bob wishes for a world where all jobs in the economy are done by AI, but humans remain militarily important, then humans will be the aristocracy in that world and lead lives of leisure and fun. If I could magically make that future happen, I would.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course it's all academic: all military jobs can probably be done by AI even sooner than other jobs. There's already surveillance and drones. But still I wanted to write it up, because so many people today think economic irrelevance is the main issue, when it isn't.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MSk9tqAmrmeasFgAZ/human-military-relevance-is-more-important-than-economic#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Human empowerment in a few fake graphs](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Goq6ZJmD9oFfHKmhu/human-empowerment-in-a-few-fake-graphs)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One view of AI’s trajectory can be captured by this “fake graph” (with the gray region reflecting roughly where we are):</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795445553/lexical_client_uploads/gvftpoqjajujap1nhxqu.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI is improving in intelligence while human intelligence is fixed. At some point AI will be so powerful that there is no way for humans to comprehend what it is doing, and hence at this point AI will necessarily take over control. The only question is whether the AI will be aligned (i.e. be a “benevolent dictator”) or misaligned (i.e., “if anyone builds it everyone dies”). Indeed, some people in the AI safety community view the former version of a “</span><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/against-against-handover"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">handover</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”, “</span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/print/scenario?plan=A&amp;supplements=0#2040-passing-the-torch-to-ais"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">handoff</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">”,  or “</span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/print/scenario?plan=A&amp;supplements=0#2040-passing-the-torch-to-ais"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">passing the torch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” to AI as a positive outcome.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I disagree with this view.  I believe that ceding control to AI is by no means necessary, and, even more importantly, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we should preserve human autonomy and sovereignty regardless of how aligned we can make AIs.</span></b></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While the biological capacity of human brains hasn’t changed in the last hundred thousand years, by any reasonable measure the intelligence of humanity as a whole has been growing. Without any biological enhancement, humanity invented concepts like writing, democracy, markets, technology, that enabled us to exercise greater control of our environment. We’ve been creating and hill climbing benchmarks, including the “build cities”,  “feed billions” and “reach the moon” benchmarks, for thousands of years.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given that, what we really should be comparing is not the intelligence of AI and an average (or even the smartest) human, but rather the intelligence of AI and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">humanity</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as a whole, and the latter quantity has been growing over time.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, humanity’s intelligence has not been growing nearly as fast as AI, and so perhaps all this argument does is buy us some extra time.  That is, the graph looks like this: (with the shaded area still corresponding to the present).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795445553/lexical_client_uploads/ebcq9xoggaiokt7jzlma.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But there is a more optimistic possibility. We have a history of enabling humans to supervise projects of growing complexity. Around the 19th century, science became complex enough so that no one person could know it all (e.g. Thomas Young, who died in 1829 is often called “the last man who knew everything”). In the 20th century, people have constructed artifacts with complexity far outstripping anything that was done before, including aircraft carriers, particle colliders, and large-scale software projects. </span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can use AI itself to expand our mechanisms to comprehend and control AI. That is, we will make sure that humans remain the key decision makers, but use AI to make the space of options legible and controllable by them. This will be “bend the humanity curve upwards” in the graph above.  But, especially with the prospect of AI being used to accelerate its own progress (aka “recursive self improvement”), I believe that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if we continue on our current path, AI’s capabilities will grow beyond our ability to comprehend and control it</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If that is the case, then using AI to control and explain AI will </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be enough to avoid takeover or handoff. Rather we would need to “bend the AI curve downwards” to achieve a graph that looks like the following:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795445553/lexical_client_uploads/hqfeizzsoxjvxxxszozw.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Crucially, we do not necessarily need to “cap” the absolute intelligence of AI, but we may need to cap its rate of progress to maintain human control. I do not have the answers as to how such mechanisms would be implemented, nor to the way to determine the precise “speed limit” for AI growth. But if we want to avoid an AI takeover or handoff, we need to be working on these questions. I suspect we will need a combination of both regulations and technical means to achieve this.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Misalignment and misuse</span></h2><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The discussions above implicitly assumed that AIs are aligned, and that they are not actively trying to destroy the world. But, realistically, some fraction of the total intelligence in the world will be misaligned or misused. So the best case we could hope for is something like:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795445553/lexical_client_uploads/dteoav0kuxysgn6v9xz4.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With the total intelligence (some function of model capabilities and inference compute) directed at aligned goals dominating that directed at misaligned goals. Much of the work of technical AI safety can be thought of as working toward increasing the gap between the two lines above.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Will that be enough? The answer depends on whether you believe in the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerable_world_hypothesis"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vulnerable World Hypothesis</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If you believe the VWH, then there is an absolute level of intelligence required to destroy the world, and when misaligned/misused AIs achieve it, this will be “game over”. </span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795445553/lexical_client_uploads/nyb28s5qaic7togkkjt7.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t believe the VWH. I think the technological level that was needed to effectively destroy humanity has increased over time. We are more resilient today than we were in the past. You can’t imagine an army killing </span><a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1016/j.euras.2016.11.004"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">possibly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> up to 10 percent of the world’s population using horses, bows, and swords, as the Mongols did in the 13th century.
