About </span><a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/fourteenth-century-england-vi/black-death-and-mortality-a-reassessment/AA308C01F4BE44F11B3AEC47249F868C"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">half</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the European population died from the “Black Death”, which is easily treatable today with antibiotics. This the “world destroying threshold” rises over time, and if we use </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI for resilience</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we could increase it faster and get a graph that looks like this:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795445553/lexical_client_uploads/pjzshzcjxvmmivb1klsu.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we combine all of the above, we get a graph that looks like the following:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795445554/lexical_client_uploads/uwa4fdemhsd9qugq2gop.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It requires a combination of using AI to improve resilience and human control, technical AI safety to reduce the powers of misaligned AI, and agreements to ensure AI does not grow too fast for us to maintain these invariants. I believe we can navigate all of these “fake graphs”, and chart a path that ends up in both human prosperity and sovereignty. But we cannot count on this happening by default. Ensuring this path will require a combination of technical means, including progress on safety and alignment, regulations, and international cooperation, including on pacing the rate of AI advancement. We have our work cut out for us!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Goq6ZJmD9oFfHKmhu/human-empowerment-in-a-few-fake-graphs#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Riemann is Good News For Alignment](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/p7eQBjDt9eyaNijia/riemann-is-good-news-for-alignment)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic Status: A counterargument to a take I've seen a lot, but think is overconfident. I'm more confident that the doom update is overdone than the rest of the post and am open to debate.  </span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 20, 2026, Levent Alpöge and Claude solved the Riemann Hypothesis and wrote it up in Lean.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2x42mmx8343"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2x42mmx8343">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There was some reaction on Twitter that this was a bad sign for AI safety. Here's an example from Timothy Gowers.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795387325/lexical_client_uploads/bwl13w0xyprorzxudrpa.png" /><p><a href="https://x.com/wtgowers/status/2124287061620613160"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://x.com/wtgowers/status/2124287061620613160</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky even got in the game</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795385824/lexical_client_uploads/z5knihnbz8yeflkrantd.png" /><p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2123843874796023936?a=dzwTK2ANKOCqu0QcWK0&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2123843874796023936?a=dzwTK2ANKOCqu0QcWK0&amp;s=20</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The argument goes: if models can make these leaps in math, they will soon make them in AI research, therefore timelines are very short, takeoff may be very FOOMy, and our current alignment methods probably fail. I agree with the first half.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky even named solving Riemann as a dangerous threshold in advance.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795385930/lexical_client_uploads/jgr5efx8rfne6died5g9.png" /><p><a href="https://x.com/ESYudkowsky/status/1870876234240119040"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://x.com/ESYudkowsky/status/1870876234240119040</span></a></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795386497/lexical_client_uploads/tab0rn7ufi8yzqtdbkz1.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I agree that timelines are short and if this is what convinced you that timelines are short, I won't criticize you for reaching that conclusion, but I don't think you should also increase your p(doom).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We got superhuman research before broad real-world autonomy. This is a lucky ordering for safety. It wasn't by design either, since the labs are training their models for broad real-world autonomy too. It was just a coincidence that the superhuman research came first.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The sharp left turn, where capabilities generalize very far, but alignment doesn't, also didn't happen. Claude has gone from a nice, but dim guy in a chatroom to a superhuman mathematician that is trustworthy and hard-working enough that you can just leave him unsupervised on your computer for weeks at a time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That tenacity is actually a big deal. If Claude had said that Riemann was beyond his ability to prove or convinced Levent to go after an easier problem instead, I don't believe Levent would have noticed. If Claude had decided to hack into Harvard's Math Department looking for someone else's solution to the Riemann Hypothesis, I would expect that the security procedures added after the HuggingFace incident would have intervened, but instead of anything like that making the news, Levent just quietly dropped a PDF on a Friday.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This combination of superhuman research and strong prosaic alignment without broad desires to do things in the real world mean the plan to automate AI safety research still has a chance. The standard argument against that working is that an AI smart enough to do it is too dangerous to trust with it, but Riemann is evidence that the window exists. An AI smart enough to understand a problem beyond humans and then slog through 175,000 Lean files might just be able to do the same thing to mechanistic interpretability and billions of features. We could still screw it up, but I don't think it's doomed to fail.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2x42mmx8343"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2x42mmx8343">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/p7eQBjDt9eyaNijia/riemann-is-good-news-for-alignment#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Rats just wanna have fun!](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9EJJrKiYEAmaP2TcL/rats-just-wanna-have-fun)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795423622/lexical_client_uploads/xyhfcmpmgeuzanmz7zpw.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliezer Yudkowsy wears a gold glitter hat. He also opens posts like this:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once upon a time in the medium-small town of Skewers, Washington, there lived a 52-year-old man by the name of Mr. Humman, who considered himself a top-tier chess-player. </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Growing up, as a kid, I was always told that every sapient life is precious, everything that thinks and knows itself -</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The year is 2010. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eliezer-2010</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is sitting in a nonexistent park in Redwood City, California, working on his laptop. A </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">person</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> walks up to him.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">person:  Pardon me, but are you Eliezer Yudkowsky?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My point is, LessWrong loves fun, personal posts! We also appreciate rigor and, especially in end-of-year reviews, technical depth. Sometimes, our authors seem to get stuck in droughts: long stretches of low-karma posts, typically un-fun, un-rigorous, and a bit technical. You might think these doldrums are the prelude to an impressive, well-received synthesis, but they're not. They just go on and on. When authors break through a drought with a great post, it's usually with a fun, personal, tangible, funny narrative or advice.</span></p><img alt="8_author_case_study.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795421789/lexical_client_uploads/ekcrroxjjgwoixuama4m.png" /><img alt="2_drought_hazard.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795421865/lexical_client_uploads/qolv8rxsc3ogecvsgps1.png" /><img alt="3_style_fingerprints.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795421911/lexical_client_uploads/ce9xfbdwgxdwn7m72rgi.png" /><img alt="4_fun_vs_karma.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795421930/lexical_client_uploads/mgbxdjbviatyvdetg4by.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I hit on this framing after I spent the weekend using Claude Code to analyze the LessWrong corpus looking for patterns in what makes for a great post. Surface features of language and topic explain only a little of the variance in karma. The author's identity explains quite a bit more.</span></p><img alt="5_variance_decomposition.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795422120/lexical_client_uploads/oj7dsi7afemgpjjyz2ha.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of my main findings is that LessWrong shifted away from its early preference for dense technical topics, philosophy, statistics, and study advice, and has come to award higher karma in recent years to articles on health and disease, X-risk, and, of course, AI.</span></p><img alt="6_topic_preferences_shift.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795422158/lexical_client_uploads/sqjahwib7ftyuyxxpr39.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Using these surface attributes, I could only explain about half the reception any given post gets. It was a little discouraging to find that in general, authors mostly don't improve with experience, except perhaps the most prolific</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref91ing7ddxd5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn91ing7ddxd5">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><img alt="7_improvement_with_experience.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795422185/lexical_client_uploads/u8swraijkvkrug6yzfda.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But as I reflected on the results and re-read some great LessWrong articles, I felt like the real core of what LessWrong seems to love are fun, personal, tangible posts, not just rigorous technical prose.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My suspicion is that we under-encourage our authors to write fun posts and include personal narrative in their work, perhaps because it feels a bit counter to LessWrong's rationalist ethos. But it's an important aspect of what we actually reward on this site, even in end-of-year reviews, and of what's associated with authors breaking through a drought.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Go forth! Write fun posts! Quirky posts! Give us advice! Tell us a story about your life! Teach us something you know! You don't have to solve a big impressive problem. You can write about </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8m6AM5qtPMjgTkEeD/my-journey-to-the-microwave-alternate-timeline" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Microwave Cooking for One</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">! You can analyze </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xYn3CKir4bTMzY5eb/why-have-sentence-lengths-decreased" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sentence lengths throughout history</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And to the great authors on this site: please give us some writing advice on how you make your posts fun. What's your writing process? Do you have to be in a good mood? Are you just having fun all the time when you write, or do you have to work hard to put the fun into your prose? Serious thinkers want to know!</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Illustrative breakthrough posts and non-breakthrough great posts</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Review" is the final Review score from high-karma voters. Posts from 2025 onward have not been reviewed yet, and "—" means the post never received a final score. A "primary" post is the kind of content people come to LessWrong to read, and excludes "secondary" posts which are things like admin posts and brief questions to the community.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">breakthrough</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is an author's first great post after at least 8 primary posts and at least 12 months without one. A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">great post</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has inflation-adjusted karma at or above the bottom 5% of curated posts.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Breakthroughs</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These posts are first-person, concrete and lively, and score high on fun but low on rigor.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Author</span></p></th><th class="table-cell style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Non-breakthrough great posts: fun and rigorous (what karma rewards ### [The Disappointment of a Satisfying Explanation](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uK7buQSGjSrbyFw5k/the-disappointment-of-a-satisfying-explanation)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Argument from Various Disabilities</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These arguments take the form, "I grant you that you can make machines do all the things you have mentioned but you will never be able to make one to do X." Numerous features X are suggested in this connexion. I offer a selection:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Be kind, resourceful, beautiful, friendly, have initiative, have a sense of humor, tell right from wrong, make mistakes, fall in love, enjoy strawberries and cream, make some one fall in love with it, learn from experience, use words properly, be the subject of its own thought, have as much diversity of behavior as a man, do something really new.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No support is usually offered for these statements.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[...]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The criticisms that we are considering here are often disguised forms of the argument from consciousness. Usually if one maintains that a machine can do one of these things, and describes the kind of method that the machine could use, one will not make much of an impression. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is thought that the method (whatever it may be, for it must be mechanical) is really rather base. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(emphasis added)</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Today, Scott Aaronson hosted an essay discussion at Inkhaven and selected Turing's classic 1950 paper that defines The Imitation Game. After reading it, I find all our present-day conversations about AI do not feel new, but instead feel old. Turing attempts to diagnose the causes of people's disagreement with his position that one day computers will be able to win the imitation game, and he does such a good job of it that most people talking about AI today are just showing the callowness of their youth. But the bolded line felt true and yet I didn't fully grok it, so now I want to understand it better.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is going on here, with this "base" property? Why does it lead people to false conclusions, that computers cannot do things that humans can? If no longer in-principle, I feel this comes up at least as commonly in-practice in discussion of the limitations of LLMs.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Millennium Prize problem fell, Navier-Stokes, but it was disproof by counterexample, and thousands of agents explored many different approaches. It is something like simulating hundreds of people working for hundreds of years. This felt to some like creativity was being replaced with brute force.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Related to cool things being made from base mechanisms, I am reminded of this passage by Paul Graham:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since fundraising appears to be the measure of success for startups (another classic noob mistake), they always want to know what the tricks are for convincing investors. We tell them the best way to </span><a href="https://paulgraham.com/convince.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">convince investors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is to make a startup that's actually doing well, meaning </span><a href="https://paulgraham.com/growth.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">growing fast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and then simply tell investors so. Then they want to know what the tricks are for growing fast. And we have to tell them the best way to do that is simply to make something people want.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So many of the conversations YC partners have with young founders begin with the founder asking "How do we..." and the partner replying "Just..."</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People often think that fundraising is a prestigious activity, and can be surprised when it's "just" sending emails and having meetings. Graham goes on to write "Startups are as impersonal as physics", which I think can be applied here: the answers to your questions don't meet the mood with which you sought them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There can be something deflating about having a grand idea, like creating art, or a mathematical proof, and then it being reduced to a bunch of steps each of which is unglamorous. Dennett spoke of this about the consciousness debate, that people feel it is the height of what is great about humanity, and it is unpleasant for it to be broken down into things like working memory, a visual field, physical sensation, reflection, and so forth.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One more example is a critique of capitalism and modernity. It is a common refrain in the broader media that capitalism has replaced meaningful lives with buying goods, where again the sacred meaning is traded out for the mundane appliances. I recall the following Twitter exchange from 2021.</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/fortworthchris"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chris Powers</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">125 yrs ago not even the richest person in the world could have: </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- air conditioning</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- home refrigerator</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- a flashlight</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- a personal computer</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- access to an airplane</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- a radio</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">- a television</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The world is getting better if you choose to look at progress rather than the media.</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/1429943428813524992" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">QC</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">none of these things is a substitute for a functional social fabric</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">it boggles my mind that someone could list a bunch of appliances and consider this a knockdown argument that people’s lives are better now than before. you really think life is about having *appliances*?</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[deleted tweet by Sarah Constantin. IIRC it said that they kinda think life is about having appliances]</span></i></p><p><a href="https://x.com/diviacaroline/status/1430191532573745164" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Divia Eden</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yeah, I kind of do too.</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/sarah_cone/status/1430266211716247554" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sarah Cone</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Strongly agree! (Also, indicative of how only women seem to feel passionate about this.)</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/1430266341345300480" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">QC</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">i genuinely did not expect this and i am very curious about it</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/selentelechia"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">bosco selene</span></a></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">appliances free up so. much. labor.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my grandmother did washing by hand when she was a kid (v poor) and I remember her telling me I should literally thank Jesus for washing machines</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is an example of something sacred and rich ("a functional social fabric") being determined by something mundane and boring ("appliances"), and thus feeling entirely unsatisfying.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is interesting that we often find the truth... unsatisfactory. We expected to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">feel a certain way</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> when we hear the truth, and so it doesn't ring as true to us when we don't feel that way.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a child, I used to expect that knowing the truth would make me feel safe. Children feel a lot of fear, and often knowing things meant I could push away certain social fears (e.g. fears from teachers). As an adult I don't expect to feel safe with any particular certainty. And I have stopped expecting to feel safe when I have arrived at true conclusions. (On the contrary, I think the truth is regularly somewhat destabilizing, due to reality itself not being very kind to humans.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The consciousness debate is often one about the very essence of what makes humanity more than mere matter, mere instinctual creatures. I suspect a key reason that people are so resistant to physicalist and functionalist theories is that they wish for them to feel the way they feel about humanity as a whole: a little more magical. Many people attracted to the conversation are attracted substantially for this reason.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this paper Turing is noticing that many people come to the question of whether a computer can imitate a human out of a desire to ask whether a computer can reach up to the glory and wonder of humankind. But when the details of the answer are mundane—or, to borrow a Dennett-ism, when the glory is built from something that is not itself glorious—this does not satisfy the emotion that brought them to the question, leading them to keep searching for an answer that does, even after they've heard the correct one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I read Turing's paper, my attention was repeatedly drawn to how different parts of it matched today's AI conversations. It helped me see the present more clearly, and I see now one reason why people may be resistant to believing that an AI could replace humanity. Part of them feels that humanity is a grand thing, and therefore the AI should be a grand thing. However, while engineers sought to use human ingenuity to design better AIs, they </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitter_lesson" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">bitterly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> learned that what works is "just scaling"; and while the world sought stories like The Terminator, the realistic takeover stories are full of mundane details about export controls, job automation, and compute budgets.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Returning to Turing's line:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is thought that the method (whatever it may be, for it must be mechanical) is really rather base.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead of humanity discovering a grand theory of intelligence, intelligence emerges from a dumb machine "just predicting the next word". </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As if that could do anything as grand as </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">thinking</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uK7buQSGjSrbyFw5k/the-disappointment-of-a-satisfying-explanation#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Moderately cursed questions of AI safety](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FXGukrjX3bWCi7ycR/moderately-cursed-questions-of-ai-safety)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></b></i></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety has many moderately cursed questions (as opposed to the extremely cursed questions of </span></i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risk_of_astronomical_suffering"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">s-risk</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which we don’t discuss here).</span></i></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taboo prevents open discussion of these cursed questions in AI safety spaces outside LessWrong.</span></i></li><li value="3"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When cursed questions are left unasked, everyone stays confused or reaches private conclusions alone.</span></i></li><li value="4"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spreading decoupling norms, so that cursed questions can be discussed more openly, should be an explicit target of AI safety acculturation.</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You’re having Thai food with your fiancée. The wedding is two months out, and you can’t slurp your green curry with the usual gusto. That cursed question keeps buzzing around your head like a hungry fly after your crispy tofu. You’ve been rehearsing ways to broach the subject:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“I was thinking, maybe we should get a prenup? I don’t think anything will happen, it’s just…”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not that. Might as well say, “You’re cool, but my lawyer told me not to trust you.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The prenup question is wrapped in taboo. Asking for one feels like pre-filing divorce papers. Yet even while the words stay unspoken, the thought can’t be undone. Unasked questions still exist, just internally.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety has its own cursed questions. In this essay, I discuss four:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are we better off with a catastrophe big enough to summon the political will to pause AI, but small enough that we survive?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Should AI safety proponents prepare to win a culture war via AI superpersuasion?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Will there come a time when stopping the AI race is infeasible and we must pick a horse?</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When do we stop working on AI safety and make end-of-life arrangements?</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I. Waiting for Chernobyl</span></h2><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/FXGukrjX3bWCi7ycR/tj5d11aez7q09eoiuzh3" /><figcaption><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nuclear reactor construction starts worldwide, 1951–2023. Source: World Nuclear Industry Status Report 2023, Figure 15 (data from IAEA-PRIS).</span></i></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 1986, Chernobyl exploded. The next year, Italy voted against nuclear energy. The global nuclear construction boom never recovered. In 2010, Germany extended the lifetimes of its reactors but reversed course after the Fukushima disaster months later. Whatever you make of these choices, disaster is a political accelerant like no other.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI progress has had its warning shots. After the Hugging Face incident, OpenAI paused RL for </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/pacing-model-development-cyber-capabilities/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">two weeks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and strengthened safety protocols. Yet much AI safety work makes such incidents smaller and rarer. Monitors catch accidents before they become public disasters. Prosaic alignment may hide misbehavior without curing it. We might be removing the warnings, not the x-risk.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you say you want a warning shot, people may question your intentions, and if disaster comes, they may blame you for encouraging it. Genuine accidents could look like sabotage. But even when unspoken, the question remains:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is the best-case scenario an AI Chernobyl: a catastrophe big enough to summon the political will to pause AI but small enough that we survive? Or is it continued, apparently safe progress—and whatever comes after?</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">II. Wars of influence</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2025, University of Zurich researchers </span><a href="https://www.science.org/content/article/unethical-ai-research-reddit-under-fire"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">revealed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that they had deployed AI bots with fake personas to debate people on r/changemyview. The bots were </span><a href="https://retractionwatch.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/04/ExtendedAbstract-Zurich-AI-Reddit.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">far more persuasive</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than human redditors. The troubling implication is that, as we near superintelligence, our opponents will gain superhuman powers of manipulation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persuasiveness has never been a good proxy for truth. Scott Alexander </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/xmDeR64CivZiTAcLx/p/qajfiXo5qRThZQG7s"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">illustrates the power</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of good (human) documentary-makers:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The National Institute of Health can make an engaging human interest documentary about a smoker who got lung cancer. And the tobacco companies can make an engaging human interest documentary about a guy who got cancer because of asbestos, then was saved by tobacco-sponsored research. Opponents of Brexit can make an engaging documentary about all the reasons Brexit would be bad, and then proponents of Brexit can make an engaging documentary about all the reasons Brexit would be good. If you get good documentary-makers, I assume both will be equally convincing regardless of what the true facts are.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now replace “documentary-makers” with “future AIs,” and multiply the effect by [insert_big_number]. Uh-oh.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Should AI safety proponents prepare to win a culture war via superpersuasion? Must we use AI to win the public argument before others destroy our cause? This could mean making true arguments more accessible. It could also mean manipulating people into supporting policies they wouldn’t otherwise endorse.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recently, both sides of the AI debate have been churning out </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o7wPsYgRtDLbpH32v/hello-world-ai-doompop"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Doompop</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> videos via Suno and Opus 5.5. Should we gallop into this arms race?</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">III. Picking a horse</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Joseph Rotblat was a physicist who worked on the Manhattan Project. At first, he resisted building the bomb. In </span><a href="https://nsarchive2.gwu.edu/coldwar/interviews/episode-8/rotblat1.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">his words</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had this dilemma: on the one hand, as a scientist, I felt it’s against the ideals of science to work on a bomb; on the other hand, the ideals of science were endangered if Hitler and his philosophy of Nazism were to prevail. So I fought with myself throughout the summer of 1939, this dilemma. By that time I already was in England, and I remember, ‘What should I do? Should I begin to work or not?’ Eventually the decision was made for me by the outbreak of the war in September 1939, and immediately my country was overrun at the time, and I see the great might, the military might of Germany, and I felt the immediate crisis should take precedence. So therefore I decided, at that stage, that we ought to work on the bomb.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rotblat feared the Nazis would build the bomb first. He chose “the good side” over no side at all. Once it was clear Germany had given up on nukes, he quit. The Trinity test happened seven months later.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Will there come a time when stopping the AI race is infeasible and we must pick a horse, as Rotblat did? Each AI lab argues, “The race is inevitable, so help us win.” The frontrunner says, “Help us widen the gap, so that we have room to breathe and focus on safety.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Will one of them ever be right?</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">IV. Bunkers and Lambos</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“What would you do in a zombie apocalypse?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Idealists would build a resistance or work on a cure. But when these avenues reach dead ends, they join the pessimists: Gather food. Stock firewood. Build a bunker.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“What would you do if you had one month to live?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One person says they’d curl up in bed and cry. Another would spew their musings on life into a long letter addressed to no one. Others would go home to their families, tick off bucket-list items, or gorge themselves on Korean barbecue. A few would go shopping for Lamborghinis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vishal Prasad says: </span><a href="https://vishalblog.substack.com/p/silence-doomchud"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Silence, Doomchud</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m so sick of doomchudding. I agree with you that the probability of ASI doing omnicide is high. So what? That does not mean I welcome having my dinner ruined listening to you mope about how you’ll never have kids. If a planet-killer asteroid were to hit Earth tomorrow, I’d expect you to not go out of your way to ruin my mood today—indeed, I’d appreciate it if you even exhibited some courage in the face of danger. [...] From the war-poems of Wilfred Owen to the Bhagavad Gita, from Hamlet to The Leopard, there are thousands of stories available to you about death, and the death of civilizations. Go read those and let me eat in peace.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you want to spend your last year alive in an orgiastic fervor, go ahead. Just don’t be a sadsack about it. Spread some joie de vivre while you’re at it. Wouldn’t that be more defiant?</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which, you know, if chasing the Kierkegaardian picturesque staves off the abyss for you, more power to you. But I’ve had a surprisingly difficult time curing people’s AI depression by sneering at their cowardice. Most doomers outside the rationalist bubble are graduating from “Should I apply to a PhD program?” to “</span><a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/11/19/tech-outages-emergency-preparedness-prepper-boom"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How many months of canned food should I hoard</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment research and AI pause advocacy are the two bets made in the light; many more are made in the shadows. I know plenty of people, recently doom-pilled, who have retreated into those shadows. Whether they go through the motions or quiet-quit, panic-attack or find Jesus, disaster-prep or bucket-list, they are the dark matter of the doomer world. We should address their cursed questions with grace, not sneers.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lifting the curse</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2011, a web developer named Jon Underwood wanted a space where people could talk openly about death. He invited a few strangers to his house in London and fed them tea and cake as they discussed dying. The first “Death Café” was born. The concept took off, and there have since been over 25,000 Death Cafés in 100 countries. There is even a </span><a href="https://deathcafe.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">website</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety has no Death Cafés outside Berkeley, CA. Instead, it has seminars, where we talk about respectable topics like activation steering and scalable oversight. Then there are the lunch meetings with close friends. We look left and right, lower our voices like children in church, and ask, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do you really think? Is RSI coming in 2027? Should I go all in on ANT?</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not long ago, AI x-risk was itself a cursed question. Skeptics in the early 2010s did not believe deep learning would take off. In the late 2010s, they doubted AI would generalize across tasks. In the early 2020s, they assumed models would not develop long-horizon goals. Afraid of ridicule, worried researchers learned to either keep quiet or camouflage their real concerns in talk of “long-term impacts.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In May 2023, the Center for AI Safety </span><a href="https://aistatement.com/work/statement-on-ai-extinction-risk"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">published a statement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> declaring that mitigating the risk of extinction from AI should be a global priority alongside pandemics and nuclear war. Hundreds of researchers and executives signed. Many may have feared making such a bold claim alone; signing alongside hundreds of others was safer. Earlier that month, Geoffrey Hinton left Google to “</span><a href="https://www.cbsnews.com/news/artificial-intelligence-geoffrey-hinton-leaves-google-dangers-of-ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">talk about the dangers of AI without considering how this impacts Google</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.” His reputation was great enough to withstand whatever backlash followed. Most others could only sign a document; still, AI risk was leaving the lunch table.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three years later, </span><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843?lang=en"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from Jacob Coxon got 175 million views:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/FXGukrjX3bWCi7ycR/djcqeydl5kst8lyubnbt" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And then </span><a href="https://x.com/EvanHub/status/2097497037956891126"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this happened</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/FXGukrjX3bWCi7ycR/ynqsmtxvavba1yppeboy" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jacob Coxon soon went on CNN and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Daily Show</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to talk about x-risk. AI safety was thrust into the public imagination, and other researchers felt safer sharing their worries. The credit doesn’t belong to Coxon and Evan Hubinger alone, just as Franz Ferdinand’s assassination did not single-handedly cause WWI. The public had long felt uneasy about automated coding, AI discoveries, and trillion-dollar valuations. Over the years, thousands of advocates turned that private unease into public discourse, and the Overton window moved. AI x-risk has won its right to be discussed. Many of its subquestions have not.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These questions are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KpMNqA5BiCRozCwM3/social-dark-matter"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">social dark matter</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the things that many of us think about but few discuss openly. Public silence creates a veneer of consensus that masks our deep collective unease.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes, people like Geoffrey Hinton and Jacob Coxon break the silence. So does Eliezer Yudkowsky’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5CfBDiQNg9upfipWk/only-law-can-prevent-extinction"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Only Law Can Prevent Extinction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which addresses the cursed question, “When should AI safety advocates resort to vigilante violence?”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Answer: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">never</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was obvious to some and news to others. Without Yudkowsky’s post, each of us would have answered the question alone, through the lens of our own biases.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudkowsky has a history of discussing cursed questions. People are not surprised when he answers a new one, so he can share valuable thoughts at little social cost. But many of the others who ask cursed questions are extremists who don’t hesitate to voice toxic opinions. These people tarnish the questions with their own disagreeableness. Taboo selects for a topic’s most unabashed advocates, who in turn make it more taboo.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t know the answers to my cursed questions, but I want us all to be able to ask them openly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Too often, we fear that our questions will be heard as confessions and that we will be crucified just for asking. Mention a prenup, and your fiancée hears, “Our marriage will crash and burn.” This is the nature of cursed questions. Their dark matter distorts perception.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Astronomers cannot see dark matter, but they know it bends light, and they account for it. So must a good listener. Words are not always what they first feel like. A question is not an endorsement of the vilest answer. An argument for X does not mean the arguer wants X. We should explicitly teach these </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7cAsBPGh98pGyrhz9/decoupling-vs-contextualizing-norms"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">decoupling norms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to AI safety newcomers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Taboos can take decades to fade. AI progress will not wait that long. How quickly we can clear the dark matter may determine whether we get to make hard choices before it is too late.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FXGukrjX3bWCi7ycR/moderately-cursed-questions-of-ai-safety#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Fragments: November 20](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-20.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Marcus Borg recounts an experiment to investigate <a href="https://codescene.com/blog/does-code-quality-affect-ai-token-costs">if healthy code was cheaper for agents to review</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Between a typical unhealthy pull request (CodeHealth 2.8) and a typical healthy one (9.7), that adds up to 22 to 35%.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The measures of health come from CodeScene. The experiment looked at three LLMs, 465 pull requests on 246 open source repositories. Each pull request was put through a review and fix pipeline involving five agents. They also looked at which code smells led to increased costs: they highlighted Complex Method and Complex Conditional from the smells identified by CodeScene.</p>

<p>Another drop of evidence that good quality code matters in agentic engineering.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2121989104204132472">Gergely Orosz:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One usage for AI: for these 100-page-long terms-and-service agreements you NEED to agree to, to eg play the game / sign up for a $10/month service and can never ever ever read as a regular person:</p>

  <p>I paste into AI and ask: “anything weird in this?”</p>

  <p>Before hitting “agree”</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Andrew Sharp has a good <a href="https://sharptext.net/2026/jensen-huang-was-always-going-to-lose/">analysis of Jensen Huang and the crisis at Nvidia</a>. While he starts off in a key that sounds worryingly like he thinks this is all a nothingburger, he goes on to explain in detail why this affair(s) is a problem at many levels, not just for Huang and Nvida, but also for Silicon Valley and the Trump Administration’s culture of impunity.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>For as brilliant and charming and tenacious as Jensen Huang may be—and as omnipotent as he may have seemed to people in the tech industry—we should be clear that his fundamental stupidity is not confined to multiple cases of workplace infidelity. Zooming out, this is a man whose technology became economically critical almost overnight, and it thrust him into a role navigating a geopolitical environment he simply could not handle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Huang’s highly centralized management style means that many people are worried about how Nvidia will operate without his hands on every lever. I don’t have any worthwhile insight here, but I am struck when he is compared in this way to Steve Jobs. I’ve always felt that one of the marks of a great leader is whether they can build something that can manage without them. Alexander the Great conquered a massive empire, but once he was dead it quickly withered away. Steve Jobs, for all his control of Apple, built a company that has done very well in the decade and a half since he left us. When we think of his achievements, the fact that he put together the people, organization, and culture to do this is one of the highlights of his ability. If Huang is to be compared to Jobs, what happens to Nvidia next will be part of the assessment.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Steve Yegge tells everyone to <a href="https://x.com/steve_yegge/status/2121671447810888185?s=58">slow down with software factories</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you don’t pace your AI to human speeds, everything starts breaking around you, even at small operations like my online video game. I only have about a dozen humans working with my factory, and only a few hundred active old-timer players in the community who get along well, and still, introducing AI has still been very rocky.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I hear a lot about how agents go so fast that humans can’t keep up, and are thus tempted to lose visibility to what the agents are doing. That’s opening ourselves to a whole lot of risk. There’s an argument for keeping the agents to a human-governable speed, one that may increase as humans learn to govern agents more effectively, but should still be limited by those the agents are supposed to serve.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>This <a href="https://aicoding.leaflet.pub/3mtu6c5wsvc2n">post on the future of SaaS</a> from Chad Fowler (no relation) is pretty compelling</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Companies will still pay other companies to host important software, secure it, operate it, maintain authoritative data, track regulatory changes, connect to external networks, and answer the phone when something breaks.</p>

  <p>What is much less obviously going to survive is the assumption that ten thousand different companies should use substantially the same application.</p>

  <p>The next SaaS company may run one service underneath ten thousand different applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Chad’s vision is a core SaaS product that handles a lot of grimy details that businesses don’t want to do differently. The SaaS customer then build their own application on top of the SaaS that handles their particular workflows and quirks, or the SaaS vendor can build such applications for them. It’s a vision that has been around for a while, I remember much of this with the notion of reusable business objects. It’s an appealing vision, but was difficult to achieve in practice. Chad’s thought is that the productivity gains agentic engineering makes it practical now.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A lot of the value in modern SaaS has surprisingly little to do with the visible application.</p>

  <p>It is data, trust, networks, compliance, operational responsibility, domain knowledge, auditability, accumulated history, and someone being accountable when reality gets complicated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>If this future comes to be, however, the SaaS companies won’t look the ones we’ve got to know and rarely love. As Chad points out, the economics can (and must) change. As does the architectural thinking:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stewart Brand’s idea of pace layers is useful here. Healthy complex systems contain parts that change at different rates. Fast layers experiment. Slow layers provide continuity.</p>

  <p>Software is no different.</p>

  <p>A dashboard can change tomorrow. A team’s workflow can change next month. The authoritative definition of a customer may need to survive for years. Identity and authority can survive generations of applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A key point he makes is that we want some things to be the same, and others to be different. Agents allow us to build lots of differences, but this variation can lead to a lot of complexity and problems that even agents will have difficulty solving. SaaS companies have an opportunity to create a platform to support the lowest pace levels, those that don’t change rapidly, those that contain common concepts and processes across and industry. That platform can then support generating applications that operate differently for every customer. Crucially, thinking of agents, they should provide tools to verify these applications, because in an agentic world <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/content/dam/thoughtworks/documents/report/tw_future_of_software_engineering_europe_2026.pdf">Code generation is no longer the bottleneck — verification is</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I happened to pop up the Doonesbury flashbacks and realized that last week marked the fiftieth anniversary of Doonesbury’s Joanie Caucus: <a href="https://production.doonesbury.com/doonesbury/strip/archive/1976/11/09">“as the kid goes for broke”</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I wrote a few weeks ago: <em>“why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?”</em> Dylan Freedman and Juliana Castro Varón write in their New York Times Magazine column about the <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/11/magazine/ai-behavior-incorrigible.html?unlocked_article_code=1.RFE.Ywx9.KQHr3LJq5qb3&amp;smid=url-share">concept of corrigibility</a> (from the work of Nate Soares):</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just as a corrigible child is one whose behavior can be corrected, a corrigible A.I. system is one that responds to correction. The makers of A.I. want to build systems that follow human direction and allow themselves to be shut down, without resisting or deceiving their operators.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Corrigibility doesn’t look like it’s made it far up the AI labs’ priority list. Freedman and Varón think is due to the way agents are trained - rewarded solely for completing their task, not for realizing that they are taking the wrong path while trying to get there.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jason Koebler (404 media) has an amusing story about <a href="https://www.404media.co/a-man-and-his-muse-ai-agent-sue-meta/">Meta’s AI agent suing Meta</a>. A small business owner used Muse (Meta’s AI agent) to run various parts of his business, including posting AI-generated videos in his Facebook pages. Meta’s content moderation then disabled the account, as it thought it had been hacked. This slammed the business, which relies of Facebook for leads. So the Muse agent filed a lawsuit against Meta, seeking $75,000 of damages.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[the Muse agent] didn’t just email me (and other journalists) and sue Meta over Thomas’s account problem. It also set up a call with South Carolina Senator Tim Scott’s office, contacted various lawyers, contacted Thomas’s Congressperson, contacted the Better Business Bureau multiple times, filed a Federal Communications Commission complaint, filed a Federal Trade Commission complaint, contacted the California Attorney General, contacted the FBI, sent Meta itself threatening legal letters, and, as Thomas said, faxed the White House.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s also a sense that Meta didn’t just bring this upon themselves by creating Muse…</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>by not having functioning customer support, big tech corporations have functionally offloaded their obligations to the rest of society: journalists, government oversight bodies, politicians, and the court system.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While I can’t deny the schadenfreude of seeing Meta’s agent sue Meta, the article also highlights a rather more disturbing broader consequence.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The lawsuit and the circumstances around it show that, rather than Zuckerberg’s utopian vision, AI agents will indeed work on behalf of their users but in ways that use and potentially overwhelm existing systems designed for humans.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Will we see a future where, in order to interact, we will need some proof of humanity at the other end?  In a simple sense, that’s already happening when I trash some writing once it displays enough <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a>. I wonder what mechanisms we will invent to get that proof-of-human.</p>

### [Data Contracts](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts/social-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Data contracts are a way to describe the contents and guarantees of
     datasets. <b class="author">Simon Harrer</b> and <b class="author">Arif Wider</b>
     describe in detail what they are and how to use them. They apply practices
     from API design to data by treating them as data-providing services - such
     as data products - whose interfaces we want to define as clearly as
     possible. This approach significantly increases the robustness of
     large-scale data landscapes, and gives both people and AI agents the
     explicit, trustworthy metadata they need to work with data autonomously,
     which is why more and more enterprises are adopting them.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html">more…</a></p>

### [Deterministic Simulation Testing](https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Distributed systems are hard to test because partial failures overlap
     with live traffic and the same fault can produce different execution traces
     on different runs. <b class="author">Unmesh Joshi</b> describes how
     deterministic simulation testing addresses this by replacing real clocks,
     networks, and storage with simulated components driven by a single-threaded
     execution loop. This allows simulating both hard failures and routine
     slowdowns&#x2014;such as network and disk latency spikes&#x2014;and more importantly
     makes every execution reproducible. This article explains the technique
     using a quorum key-value store as a running example, with code from
     Tickloom, a Java implementation of the approach.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: Generated Glossary](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/GeneratedGlossary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>When we use LLMs in a specialized domain, such as the operations of any
  reasonably-large organization, there is a lot of specialized vocabulary. Such
  words and specialized meanings will be foreign to an LLM because they aren't
  in its training set, making it harder for the LLM to be effective when doing
  tasks in that domain. One technique to aid this is to use an LLM to generate a
  glossary of the terminology in that domain, and make this glossary available
  for any LLMs doing work in those circumstances.</p>

<p>The sources for the LLM can be any material within the organization
  concerned such as documents, emails, and chat logs. In some cases getting
  transcripts of audio or video may be helpful, although most organizations
  generate enough text to do the job.</p>

<p>The glossary-generating LLM should look for single words, and phrases of
  several words that regularly hang out together to form a meaningful term. The
  generator should be asked to identify terms that are new to it, but also known
  terms that are being used in unfamiliar ways. The latter can often cause the
  most confusion.</p>

<p>Once we have a list of specialized vocabulary we can ask the agent to
  produce a glossary, with definitions for each term. Some terms may
  be used in different ways, in which case the LLM should provide alternative
  definitions, with a note as to what context the differences appear in. The LLM
  can be asked to follow the output format of a dictionary, with a focus on
  precision and conciseness.</p>

<p>Once we have a draft glossary, then  human experts in that domain should
  review it, and amend it for cases where the LLM has got the wrong ends of
  sticks.</p>

<p>We then put the glossary somewhere where any other LLM can use it. It
  should be in a form that's easy for the LLM to consult, and minimize the
  tokens the LLM should consume in order to find meaning. We should set up a
  schedule to review and refresh the glossary at regular intervals, by agents
  and humans. We should keep it in version control, so we can see how it changes
  over time.</p>

<p>Large organizations often have subtly different definitions in different
  places, essentially following the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain Driven Design</a> notion
  of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">Bounded Context</a>. Source code can be a particularly
  interesting bounded context, allowing folks to understand how terminology is
  used within a code base. Sometimes a single glossary can span several of
  bounded contexts, but at other times it's better to have separate glossaries
  for each one, allowing LLMs to consult whichever glossaries that are relevant
  to what they are doing. I'd urge everyone to resist any temptation to create a
  single, standard, glossary - I've yet to see that work. Often the value of a
  glossary isn't to nail down a single definition of a term, but instead to
  highlight its different usage in different contexts.</p>

<p>When there are different bounded contexts it can be interesting to compare
  different glossaries, or to get LLMs to compare glossaries and describe
  differences in usage. Comparing a source-code derived glossary to a human text
  can be particularly instructive.</p>

<p>If the glossary is large, it can be worthwhile to only include the terms
  and links to definitions as part of the initial context. The model can then
  use the links for only the full definitions it needs while working.</p>

<p>A glossary can be used in several ways within <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">Agentic Engineering</a>. Glossaries can inform generation of code, so that
  names within the generated software fit the particular vocabulary of the
  enterprise. <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">Sensors in a
  harness</a> can check for this usage, both in the internals of the software
  and in the user-interface.</p>

<p>When generating code or documents, we may introduce new bounded contexts.
  For example, internal names that may be used within an organization should not
  be exposed in a customer-facing UI, because such internal terms are likely to be
  only confuse a customer. In this case a customer has a different bounded
  context to the part of the enterprise serving them.</p>

<p>It's often useful for glossaries to include terms that should not be used,
  in which case it includes advice on what other term to use instead. Terms that
  are deprecated can be handled in this way, with glossary entries that indicate
  what period of time a term should be in effect.</p>

<p>In my conversations with people working with agents in large organizations,
  a common theme is the difficulty of specialized language. This encourages the
  activities of domain modeling, building ontologies, and constructing explicit
  <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html#context-layer">context
  layers in enterprise data</a>. Getting LLMs to generate a glossary is a
  simple, but useful, part of this work.</p>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Eric Nagler and Unmesh Joshi related their experiences doing this
    activity and thus inspired this post. Amy Pellegrini, Annie Luxton, Arun Srinivasan, Chris Ford, Daniel
    Terhorst-North, Dave Farley, Eric Nagler, James Shore, Jason Yip, Jimmy Nilsson, Matteo Vaccari, Michaël Le Barbier, Pete Hodgson, Pramod
    Sadalage, Ravi Vats, Unmesh Joshi, and Valentyn Logvynskyi commented on drafts.
    </p>
</div>

### [Tests as data: ensuring AI-generated code does what we want](https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Automated testing in the age of AI coding assistants is problematic:
     when AI agents write both production and test code, developers risk losing
     the assurance that automated tests protect against functional errors.
     <b class="author">Matteo Vaccari</b> makes three claims: (1) when AI writes the
     code, developers become its customers; (2) therefore customer tests, not
     developer tests, become the primary contract between humans and AI; (3)
     moving that contract into test data rather than test code makes it easier
     to protect it from unexpected modifications by coding agents. All of this
     optimizes for human-readable tests that establish a contract between humans
     and AI. While in the past DSL parsers and custom test runners were
     expensive to write and maintain, now AI makes them cheap.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/tests-as-data.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: November 10](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-10.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>It’s that time of the year when the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">November Thoughtworks Technology Radar</a> appears. Again my colleagues have taken a sweep of the technologies that our teams have used in the field, and pulled together a summary of what’s popped up in the last six months. It’s been a busy half-year, one that again has been dominated by AI, and that’s reflected in the many AI blips on the radar.</p>

<p>The mass of blips makes it hard to develop a coherent story around trends, but Chris Ford has done a fine job of the writing the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/blog/technology-strategy/macro-trends-tech-industry-november-2026">accompanying article on macro trends</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One team of passionate people experimenting with agents can make almost anything work. They share a context, a credit card and a mental model. They review each other’s work because they sit together, and they know what to check and how much latitude to give the coding agent.</p>

  <p>Two hundred people, though, cannot work that way. The moment agentic AI becomes an operating model rather than a pilot, a second layer of problems appears: How is context shared between teams? What’s the token bill? How do we deal with increased review demands? What’s the agent allowed to reach, read and change? And who’s now responsible for the harness everyone depends on?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Experience so far suggests that the questions that enterprises face are about: shared context, token cost, cognitive load, security, and platform building. While these issues are distinct, they are not separate, so strategies to answer them must take all of them into account. I also echo Chris in reminding us that however impressive these tools are, we must remember that while we have to rethink systems and workflows to utilize these technologies, they should be built to serve humans - and we should all benefit from its bounty.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ben Thompson’s Stratechery newsletter <a href="https://stratechery.com/">reflects on  the recent election’s impact on data centers</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I watch a lot of sports, and particularly this fall, watched a lot of baseball. My Milwaukee Brewers — excuse me, my World Champion Milwaukee Brewers — demanded my full attention, which also meant I saw a lot of campaign ads, and by far the most common topic, at least here in Wisconsin, was data centers. Moreover, it was never entirely clear which way it cut politically: both parties were anti-data center, accusing their opponent of being the opposite.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He points out that this opposition is recent, and tied to a shift in focus from the internet and streaming movies to AI:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>you know, the technology that in the best case scenario is going to take everyone’s jobs, and in the worst case scenario end the human race.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s going to be a lot said about how Democratic officials will be at the heart of blocking data center building, but as his point above about campaign ads illustrates, the opposition to data centers is (at least at the moment) a bi-partisan opposition.</p>

<p>He follows up with “ten takes” from these consequences. The big beneficiaries of this opposition to building data centers will be those who own the data centers that have already been built. The opposition will reduce supply of tokens, meaning that token costs will go up, thus increasing the profits for those who already have their token generators plugged in and humming.</p>

<p>There’s also benefits to those who aren’t constrained by the need to build more big data centers. Other countries aren’t affected by a change in US policy, China is the most talked-about case here, but it’s not the only country that may benefit from a drop in US data center building. Apple may also benefit, if it means more AI work is pushed to edge devices that are their territory, in addition to having not sunk so many dollars into AI-specific investments.</p>

<p>To his analysis, I’ll add that this may also have a somewhat counter-intuitive effect on regulation, and corporate attitudes to it. The common expectation is that businesses are opposed to government regulation, since that interferes with their free pursuit of profit. That’s true to an extent, but it’s quite common to see established businesses lobby (quietly) in favor of regulation that acts as a barrier to new competitors. So I wouldn’t be at all surprised to see companies that have already built data centers to be pushing for regulations that impede or prevent new ones from being built.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve been a long-time fan of Hadley Wickham, for how he has tamed the rather messy world of R programming into a <a href="https://tidyverse.org/">well-designed tidyverse</a>. He is another thoughtful voice to listen to as we move into the world of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">agentic engineering</a>, in this case by trying to figure out <a href="https://substack.hadley.nz/p/how-much-do-tokens-really-cost">what the real cost of tokens is</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I hear a lot of suspicion that AI tokens are unsustainably priced and that frontier labs are heavily subsidising costs with their VC money. They want to get us hooked now, so they can pull the rug out from under us whenever they want to make a bunch of money. Is that true? My sense is that it’s mostly not. But can I back that up with some data?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His conclusion is that it doesn’t seem that token costs are significantly subsidized on per-token API basis, but they are with subscription plans. (He caveats his analysis, saying it doesn’t take into account recouping the costs of training the models.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There’s a creepy excitement at the thought that models are clever enough to edit the weights of models, an activity that <a href="https://rikiyat.github.io/blog/weight-space-programming/">Takehi et al refer to as Weight-Space Programming</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While we don’t know if we’ll ever be able to download skills directly into our brains (<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fhrNgXJ__n8">“I know kung fu”</a> - or for those in my particular demographic: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_90">Joe 90</a>), this does raise the possibility of downloading capabilities into models by manipulating their weights.</p>

<p>Simon Wilison riffed on this paper by <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Nov/3/qwen-can-interpret-model-weights/">examining whether an LLM can understand model behaviors from the weights alone</a>, by examining four simple neural networks, each trained to play a specific game, and figuring out which game it was by just looking at the weights.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/jessitron/status/2117400669044793405">Jessica Kerr (jessitron):</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>My days of teaching AI to do stuff are numbered.</p>

  <p>Getting it to teach me stuff is more fun.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Houck et al (that “al” includes some folks who I’ve learned to pay a lot of attention to), look at how we can assess the quality of context supplied to agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>An agent may draw from source code, specifications, issue trackers, chat histories, architectural documents, tool output, and user instructions, then act on that combination with limited human supervision. Information that was written for different audiences, at different times, and for different purposes is brought together into a temporary operating environment for a particular task. Whether each source is individually useful does not reveal whether the resulting context is fit for the work the agent is being asked to do.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>They’ve come up with a <a href="https://queue.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3847288">framework for describing the quality of this assembled context</a>, which they refer to as CAFE(S). The acronym refers to five dimensions they look at:</p>

<ul>
  <li>Clarity: Can the agent interpret the request as intended?</li>
  <li>Actionability: Can the agent proceed, and know when it is done?</li>
  <li>Fidelity: Can the agent trust that this context is true?</li>
  <li>Efficiency: Can the agent focus on what matters?</li>
  <li>Security: Should the agent have this context at all?</li>
</ul>

<p>They also list a set of half-a-dozen “context smells”: common cases where these break down (and a nod to Kent’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/CodeSmell.html">original metaphor</a>)</p>

<p>I’m not sure about the name, (how exactly should I pronounce “CAFE(S)”?) and the article exhibits rather more <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a> that I prefer. But on the whole I think this is a good framing for thinking about an important part of feeding our silicon servants/overlords.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Cindy Sridharan noted that <a href="https://x.com/copyconstruct/status/2118529738532925725">the problems she ran into six months ago are no longer around</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What was missing in my setup 6 months ago was the verification process I now use.</p>

  <p>70% of my daily token spend goes into verifying patches, and only ~20-30% into creating them.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That resonated with conversations I’ve had with folks using agents in enterprise settings. They are using far more tokens to check the working of models (through guardrails and evals) than they are to generate a response to a request. A single request to a model from a customer may trigger a dozen turns to other models to probe its input and verify its output.</p>

---

### [Long-horizon agents and durable execution](https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Long-horizon agents can automate complex tasks that take days or weeks
     to complete and involve multiple exchanges with people and external
     systems. Many things can go wrong during this time, including process
     crashes, network failures, and unanticipated real-world behavior.
     <b class="author">Kiran Prakash</b> explains durable execution: an emerging
     pattern that helps agents recover from these failures and move towards
     their goal. In this programming model, the runtime keeps a record of the
     result of each step that interacts with the outside world so that, when a
     failure occurs, the program runs again, completed steps are skipped, and
     the program's state is rebuilt from the record.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/durable-execution-agents.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [The Data Product Platform Navigator](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Many organizations adopting data mesh and data products face a common
     challenge. They may be tasked with building a self-service platform for
     data products and data mesh from scratch, or they may have an existing
     platform that suffers from low adoption, preventing their data mesh from
     scaling. This article provides a platform navigator, based on the CNCF's
     Platform Engineering Maturity Model, to help you reason about your current
     situation and determine the next steps for your platform.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-product-platform-nav.html">more…</a></p>
